BRITISH TRIBUTE 
"10. CHINA” 


BrtTONS and ‘Chinese. alike 
wil! welcome. the warm 
and sincere expression of 
sympathy voiced by Lord 
Chatfield, H.M. Minister for 
the Co-ordination of Defence, 
at the annual dinner of the 
China Society in London last 
Wednesday. An admiral by 
training and a man, who pre-- 
fers action to words, Lord 
Chatfield is not accustomed. 
to making flowery spteches of 
eulogy... When he says & 
thing, he means it, Hence, 


his sincere and frank words? 
must be taken not as a mere |’ 


gesture but-a true indication 
of the trend of public feeling 
throughout, the British Em- 
pire and ofthe desire of the 
British people. to strengthen 
the bonds of friendship which 


‘fexist between the great wes- 


tern Democracy and the great 
‘Eastern Republic. His 
graceful tribute to the 
Chinese people and to their, 
great leader, Generalissimo 
Chiang Kai-shek, is exceed- 
ingly. gratifying and his words 
of comfort ‘for the Chinese 
people in. their Jone struggle 
against violent unprovoked 
aggression by 2 unscrupulous 


invader will find the fullest | 


support .among all 


self- 
respecting Britons, ‘ 


COMING as they do from a 


member of the ‘British Gov- 
ernment, such -fervent and 
friendly words will undoubt- 


| edly carry considerable. weight, 


and (as. the ‘Manchester 
. Tightly. ointed 


out) will never be * 


will ‘be glad 


from a member of Mr..Cham- | 
—|beriain’s Cabinet that our 


;sympathy is based not solely 
on the extent of our economic 
interests. ; 

WITH . Lord: Chatfield’s 
words “In this.timé of trial, 
we have the greatest admira- 
tidn for the way you -re-| 
luctantly laid aside the pen 
to girt the sword” all Britons. 
will - wholeheartedly - concur,’: 
|while the Chinese people will i 
and consolation, in his con- | 
fident statement, “But we be- 
Heve .that, dust as the man 
who wrestled with trials, will 
be ‘stronger for having over- 
come them, so China will : 
come: through the ‘struggle | 
with new experience and new} 
courage.”

DEMOCRATIC POLICY 


“Had the Democratic. Pow- 
ers adopted such a policy long 
ago. to mark their refusal to - 
acknowledge the right of any 
Power to Invade’ a . weaker. 

hbour, China -' would ‘not | 
ave been desolated; occident- 
al prestige in Asia ‘would not 
have been overthrown; small 
nations in Europe “would © not 
have been robbed of. their in- 
dependence, and’ ‘the world 
would not have been consront- 
ed, as it ts to-day, with the 


most colossal calamity that has . 


ever menaced {ts people... -,, 
. “f6 help China now to crip- 
ple Japan would be to help De- 
mocratic nations to": preserve 
« thelr entities and thelr peace, 
“Jo continue to help Japan ¢ 
with materials of ’war- to de- 
vastate and conquer China ‘will 
‘ be to sow dragon's teeth in all 
corners of the earth, which 


-will arise to crush liberty and -- 


destroy civilization.”

sors.

and Safety of the country to the 
,mercy: and soe of others,” he

that electricity was now in use in 
8,000,000 homes In, this country and 
additions were being’ made at a 
more rapid rate than elsewhere. 
Large scale . electrification had 
begun late owlng to the mature 
state of ‘industrial development 
based on steam, but' the position 
had. been.. profoundly " changed 
during the past ten years. New 
British industries ; based entirely 
on: electricity had been created 
and electricity in city homes was 
las cheap as anywhere In the world,

U. s. Cc otton. For 
Rubher And Tin 


‘LONDON, May 6 (Reuter)—The 
“Pinanclal. News,”. discussing the 
Anglo-American commodity barter 
plan, says that commodity market 
cirelés belleve .the United States 
War. Department’ requires war 
reserves of between" 200,000 and 
360,000 tons, of rubber and’ from 


| 80,000 to 75,000 tons"of tin. 


On: this basis, the Value of ma~ 
terial involved would be between 
£25,000,000 and £40,000,0000. 

Production of a reserve of 300,- 
000 tons of rubber’ would require| 
an additional quota of 2 ‘per cent. 
for one year. | + 

For 9 tin’ reserve of 65, 000 tons 
would’ need an. extra quota of 30 
per cent. 

“It appears that the bul ot ‘the 
payment for rubber and tin would 


-) be- made in cotton, 


Lowest figure mentioned fs “2 
000,000° bales, which . represents 
about two years" British consump- 
tion of United States cotton, 


-The 7,000,000 rafiroad ties which 
willbe required by the Canad: 
fan National Railways ‘during 
1939 would,make a continuous 
line, if placed end.to end, 10,600 
milés long or’ almost three times 
the disttince between Halifax, 
Canada's’ most easterly port’ and 
Vaneauser’ on the Pacific Coast.

in’ uses.

vidual’ units js being steadily re- 


duced, the number ts Nkely to In-: 


crease in the near-future,

nent on the home production of

branch of engineering or as a’).
